

# ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY LAB

Engineering Chemistry lab is an essential component of the engineering curriculum. It provides a platform for students to understand the basic principles of chemistry and their applications in engineering. Here are some key points to keep in mind while studying for the Engineering Chemistry Lab exams:

## Safety Precautions

Chemistry labs can be hazardous, and safety precautions must be taken seriously. Here are some safety precautions that must be followed in the Engineering Chemistry lab:

- Wear appropriate lab attire, including lab coat, gloves, and safety glasses.
- Handle chemicals with care and make sure to read the safety instructions before starting any experiment.
- Keep the lab workspace clean and organized to avoid accidents.
- Always use a fume hood when handling volatile chemicals.
- In case of any accidents or emergencies, inform the lab supervisor immediately.

## Laboratory Techniques

In Engineering Chemistry Lab, students learn various laboratory techniques that are essential for conducting experiments. Here are some of the laboratory techniques that you should be familiar with:

- Titration: A process of measuring the concentration of a solution by adding a reagent that reacts with the solute.
- Distillation: A method of separating a mixture of two or more liquids based on their boiling points.
- Chromatography: A method of separating and analyzing chemical compounds based on their properties.
- Spectroscopy: A technique used to study the interaction between matter and electromagnetic radiation.

## Laboratory Experiments

Engineering Chemistry Lab involves performing experiments to understand the principles of chemistry and their applications in engineering. Here are some of the common experiments that you will perform in the lab:

- Acid-base titration: A technique used to determine the concentration of an acid or base solution.
- Synthesis of aspirin: A process of synthesizing aspirin from salicylic acid and acetic anhydride.
- Identification of unknown compounds: A process of identifying the unknown compounds using various laboratory techniques.
- Electroplating: A process of depositing a metal coating onto a metal surface using an electric current.

## Lab Reports

Lab reports are an essential component of the Engineering Chemistry lab. You will be required to submit a lab report for each experiment that you perform in the lab. Here are some key points to keep in mind while writing a lab report:

- The report should include a brief introduction to the experiment and its objectives.
- The report should include a detailed description of the experimental procedure, including any observations and measurements.
- The report should include a discussion of the results and their implications.
- The report should include a conclusion that summarizes the findings of the experiment and their significance.

Engineering Chemistry Lab is an important subject that requires both theoretical and practical knowledge. By following the safety precautions, mastering laboratory techniques, performing experiments, and writing lab reports, you can excel in this subject and apply the principles of chemistry in engineering.

### 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments.

In this section, we will cover the basic concepts and principles of analytical instruments. Understanding these fundamental concepts is crucial for interpreting and analyzing experimental data accurately. 

Here are the key points that we will cover:

- **Measurement**: Analytical instruments are designed to measure physical or chemical properties of a sample, such as mass, volume, temperature, pH, conductivity, etc.

- **Accuracy**: Accuracy is the ability of an instrument to provide measurements that are close to the true value. It is affected by factors such as calibration, sensitivity, and precision.

- **Precision**: Precision is the degree of repeatability or reproducibility of a measurement. It is affected by factors such as instrument design, sample preparation, and data analysis.

- **Sensitivity**: Sensitivity is the smallest detectable change in the measured property. It is affected by factors such as signal-to-noise ratio, resolution, and dynamic range.

- **Selectivity**: Selectivity is the ability of an instrument to measure a specific property in the presence of other interfering substances. It is affected by factors such as sample preparation, analytical method, and instrument design.

- **Signal**: The signal is the measurable response of the instrument to the sample. It can be a physical, chemical, or electrical property.

- **Noise**: Noise is the random variation in the signal that is not related to the sample. It can be caused by external factors such as temperature, humidity, or electromagnetic interference.

- **Calibration**: Calibration is the process of adjusting the instrument to ensure accurate and precise measurements. It involves comparing the instrument response to a known standard or reference material.

- **Validation**: Validation is the process of verifying that the instrument and analytical method are suitable for the intended use. It involves testing the instrument performance on a range of samples with known properties.

By understanding these fundamental concepts, students will be able to select the appropriate analytical instrument for a given application, design experiments, and interpret the experimental results accurately.



### 2. Analysis of Chloride Content, Hardness, and Alkalinity of Water

Water is an essential natural resource, and its quality is crucial for human consumption and other purposes. The analysis of water quality parameters, such as chloride content, hardness, and alkalinity, is necessary to ensure that the water is safe for use. In this section, we will discuss the methods and importance of analyzing these parameters.

#### 2.1 Chloride Content Analysis

Chloride is a common anion found in water, and its concentration is usually measured in milligrams per liter (mg/L). The chloride content in water can indicate the presence of saltwater intrusion or contamination from industrial or agricultural activities. The following methods are used to analyze chloride content in water:

- Titration method: This involves the addition of silver nitrate solution to a water sample until all the chloride ions react with the silver ions. The endpoint is reached when there is no more silver ion left to react with the chloride ion. The amount of silver nitrate used is then calculated to determine the chloride content.
- Ion-selective electrode method: This involves the use of a specialized electrode that reacts with chloride ions and generates an electrical signal. The electrical signal is then measured to determine the chloride concentration.

#### 2.2 Hardness Analysis

Water hardness refers to the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water, usually measured in parts per million (ppm). Hard water can cause scaling in pipes and appliances, as well as affect the taste of water. The following methods are used to analyze water hardness:

- Titration method: This involves the addition of a chelating agent to a water sample that reacts with calcium and magnesium ions. The endpoint is reached when all the calcium and magnesium ions have reacted with the chelating agent. The amount of chelating agent used is then calculated to determine the hardness of the water.
- Atomic absorption spectroscopy: This involves the use of a specialized instrument that measures the absorption of light by calcium and magnesium ions in a water sample. The amount of light absorbed is then used to determine the hardness of the water.

#### 2.3 Alkalinity Analysis

Alkalinity is a measure of the buffering capacity of water, which refers to its ability to neutralize acids. Alkalinity is usually measured in ppm of calcium carbonate (CaCO3). The following methods are used to analyze alkalinity:

- Titration method: This involves the addition of an acid to a water sample until the alkalinity is neutralized. The amount of acid used is then calculated to determine the alkalinity of the water.
- Colorimetric method: This involves the use of a specialized instrument that measures the color change of a water sample when a specific reagent is added. The color change is then used to determine the alkalinity of the water.

In conclusion, the analysis of chloride content, hardness, and alkalinity of water is essential to ensure that the water is safe for human consumption and other purposes. The methods discussed in this section are commonly used in water quality analysis and are important for students to understand.



### 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of a liquid.

In this section, we will discuss the measurement of pH, surface tension, and viscosity of a liquid. These three properties are important in understanding the behavior of a liquid and can be useful in various applications.

#### pH Measurement

1. pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a solution is.
2. The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, with 7 being neutral, values below 7 are acidic, and values above 7 are basic.
3. The pH of a solution can be measured using a pH meter or pH paper.
4. A pH meter is an electronic device that measures the voltage difference between a pH electrode and a reference electrode.
5. pH paper is a strip of paper that changes color based on the pH of the solution it is dipped in.
6. pH measurements are important in many fields, including chemistry, biology, and environmental science.

#### Surface Tension Measurement

1. Surface tension is a measure of the force required to break the surface of a liquid.
2. It is caused by the attraction between molecules on the surface of the liquid.
3. Surface tension can be measured using a tensiometer.
4. A tensiometer is a device that measures the force required to break the surface of a liquid.
5. Surface tension measurements are important in understanding the behavior of liquids, such as in the formation of bubbles or the spreading of a liquid on a surface.

#### Viscosity Measurement

1. Viscosity is a measure of a liquid's resistance to flow.
2. It is caused by the friction between molecules in the liquid.
3. Viscosity can be measured using a viscometer.
4. A viscometer is a device that measures the time it takes for a liquid to flow through a narrow tube.
5. Viscosity measurements are important in many fields, including engineering, chemistry, and medicine.

In conclusion, the measurement of pH, surface tension, and viscosity of a liquid are important in understanding the behavior of liquids and can be useful in various applications. The methods for measuring these properties are well-established and widely used in many fields of science and engineering.



### 4. Preparation of Different Resins

Resins are synthetic or natural substances that are widely used in various industries, such as construction, automotive, and electronics, for their unique properties. The preparation of different resins involves several steps, including selecting the right raw materials, mixing them in the correct proportions, and curing the mixture to form a solid material. In this section, we will discuss the preparation of some commonly used resins.

#### 4.1 Polyester Resin

Polyester resin is a thermosetting plastic that is widely used in the production of fiberglass reinforced plastics (FRP) and other composite materials. The preparation of polyester resin involves the following steps:

1. Select the raw materials: The primary raw materials used in the preparation of polyester resin are unsaturated polyester resin, styrene monomer, and a catalyst.

2. Mix the raw materials: The raw materials are mixed in the correct proportions using a mixer. The resin and styrene monomer are mixed together, followed by the addition of the catalyst.

3. Curing the mixture: The mixture is then allowed to cure at room temperature or with the application of heat. The curing process results in the formation of a solid material that is suitable for various applications.

#### 4.2 Epoxy Resin

Epoxy resin is a thermosetting plastic that is widely used in various industries for its excellent adhesive and mechanical properties. The preparation of epoxy resin involves the following steps:

1. Select the raw materials: The primary raw materials used in the preparation of epoxy resin are an epoxy resin and a hardener.

2. Mix the raw materials: The raw materials are mixed in the correct proportions using a mixer. The epoxy resin and hardener are mixed together to form a homogeneous mixture.

3. Curing the mixture: The mixture is then allowed to cure at room temperature or with the application of heat. The curing process results in the formation of a solid material that is suitable for various applications.

#### 4.3 Polyurethane Resin

Polyurethane resin is a thermosetting plastic that is widely used in various industries for its excellent mechanical and chemical properties. The preparation of polyurethane resin involves the following steps:

1. Select the raw materials: The primary raw materials used in the preparation of polyurethane resin are polyol and diisocyanate.

2. Mix the raw materials: The raw materials are mixed in the correct proportions using a mixer. The polyol and diisocyanate are mixed together to form a homogeneous mixture.

3. Curing the mixture: The mixture is then allowed to cure at room temperature or with the application of heat. The curing process results in the formation of a solid material that is suitable for various applications.

In conclusion, the preparation of different resins involves selecting the right raw materials, mixing them in the correct proportions, and curing the mixture to form a solid material. The properties of the final product depend on the type of raw materials used and the curing conditions. Students studying the properties and applications of different resins must have a good understanding of their preparation process.



### 5. Synthesis of Organic Compounds: Adipic Acid and Paracetamol

Organic compounds are molecules that contain carbon atoms bonded with other elements, such as hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, or halogens. They are essential for life and can be found in a wide range of products, from food and medicine to plastics and fuels. In this section, we will discuss the synthesis of two organic compounds: adipic acid and paracetamol.

#### Adipic Acid

Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid that is commonly used in the production of nylon fibers and resins. It can be synthesized by two routes: conventional and green.

##### Conventional Route

The conventional route for adipic acid synthesis involves the following steps:

1. Oxidation of cyclohexane with nitric acid to form cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol.
2. Oxidation of cyclohexanone with nitric acid to form adipic acid.

This process requires high temperatures and pressures, and generates large amounts of waste and greenhouse gases.

##### Green Route

The green route for adipic acid synthesis aims to reduce the environmental impact of the conventional process by using renewable resources and minimizing waste. It involves the following steps:

1. Catalytic oxidation of cyclohexene with hydrogen peroxide to form cyclohexene oxide.
2. Hydrolysis of cyclohexene oxide with water to form adipic acid.

This process requires lower temperatures and pressures, and generates less waste and greenhouse gases.

#### Paracetamol

Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a widely used pain reliever and fever reducer. It can be synthesized by two routes: conventional and green.

##### Conventional Route

The conventional route for paracetamol synthesis involves the following steps:

1. Nitration of phenol with nitric acid to form 4-nitrophenol.
2. Reduction of 4-nitrophenol with sodium borohydride to form 4-aminophenol.
3. Acetylation of 4-aminophenol with acetic anhydride to form paracetamol.

This process requires the use of toxic and hazardous chemicals, and generates large amounts of waste.

##### Green Route

The green route for paracetamol synthesis aims to reduce the environmental impact of the conventional process by using safer and more sustainable methods. It involves the following steps:

1. Hydrogenation of nitrobenzene with hydrogen gas over a palladium catalyst to form aniline.
2. Acetylation of aniline with acetic anhydride to form paracetamol.

This process uses less hazardous chemicals, generates less waste, and has a higher atom economy.

In conclusion, the synthesis of organic compounds such as adipic acid and paracetamol can be achieved by both conventional and green routes. While the conventional routes may have higher yields and efficiency, the green routes are more sustainable and environmentally friendly. It is important for students to understand both methods and their advantages and disadvantages in order to make informed decisions about their future research and career paths.



## LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

Here is a list of experiments that are commonly conducted in science and engineering:

1. Acid-Base Titration: This experiment is used to determine the concentration of an acid or a base solution by titrating it against a standardized solution of the opposite type.

2. Spectrophotometry: This experiment is used to measure the absorbance or transmission of light by a sample in the ultraviolet, visible or infrared regions of the electromagnetic spectrum.

3. Chromatography: This experiment is used to separate and analyze the components of a mixture based on their differences in chemical properties such as solubility, polarity, or size.

4. Electrolysis: This experiment is used to split a compound into its constituent elements using an electric current.

5. Enzyme Kinetics: This experiment is used to study the rate of enzyme-catalyzed reactions by measuring the change in concentration of the substrate or the product over time.

6. DNA Extraction: This experiment is used to isolate DNA from cells or tissues for further analysis or manipulation.

7. Microscopy: This experiment is used to observe and analyze objects that are too small to be seen by the naked eye, such as cells, microorganisms, or particles.

8. Newton's Laws: This experiment is used to study the fundamental principles of motion and force described by Isaac Newton's laws.

9. Boyle's Law: This experiment is used to study the relationship between the pressure and volume of a gas at a constant temperature, as described by Boyle's law.

10. Ohm's Law: This experiment is used to study the relationship between the voltage, current, and resistance of an electrical circuit, as described by Ohm's law.

These experiments are fundamental to many fields of science and engineering and can provide valuable insights into the workings of the world around us.



### 1. Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

Analytical equipment and apparatus are used in various scientific fields to obtain accurate results. Calibration is an essential process to ensure that the equipment is providing accurate and reliable results. Here are some key points to keep in mind while calibrating analytical equipment and apparatus:

1. Calibration is the process of comparing the readings of the equipment against a standard reference. The reference should be traceable to a recognized standard like NIST (National Institute of Standards and Technology).

2. Calibration should be performed regularly to ensure that the equipment is providing accurate results. The frequency of calibration depends on the type of equipment and how frequently it is used. Generally, it is recommended to calibrate the equipment after a specific number of measurements or after a particular time interval.

3. Calibration should be performed by trained professionals who have the necessary knowledge and experience to carry out the process correctly.

4. The calibration process involves adjusting the equipment to match the reference standard. The adjustment should be made according to the manufacturer's instructions and should be recorded for future reference.

5. The calibration process should be documented in detail, including the date of calibration, the name of the person who performed the calibration, the reference standard used, and the results obtained.

6. The calibration records should be kept for future reference and should be easily accessible. These records can be used to demonstrate compliance with regulatory requirements and to identify any issues with the equipment.

7. It is also essential to perform regular maintenance of the equipment to ensure that it is functioning correctly. Maintenance activities may include cleaning, replacing worn parts, and lubrication.

8. If the equipment is found to be out of calibration, it should not be used until it has been recalibrated. Using equipment that is out of calibration can lead to inaccurate results and can compromise the quality of the data.

In conclusion, calibration is an essential process to ensure the accuracy and reliability of analytical equipment and apparatus. It should be performed regularly by trained professionals and documented in detail. Regular maintenance of the equipment is also necessary to ensure that it is functioning correctly. By following these guidelines, we can obtain accurate and reliable results from our analytical equipment and apparatus.



### 2. Determination of Hardness of water sample by EDTA method.

Hardness of water is an important parameter to determine the quality of water. It is caused by the presence of dissolved minerals such as calcium and magnesium ions. Hard water can cause scaling in pipes, decrease soap efficiency, and affect the taste of water. Hence, it is important to determine the hardness of water before it is used for any purpose. EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) method is a commonly used method to determine the hardness of water. This method involves the use of a complexometric titration to determine the amount of calcium and magnesium ions present in the water sample.

The following are the steps involved in the determination of hardness of water sample by EDTA method:

1. Take a water sample of known volume in a conical flask. Add a few drops of Eriochrome Black T indicator to the flask. The indicator will form a complex with calcium and magnesium ions present in the water sample.

2. Titrate the water sample with EDTA solution. The EDTA will react with the calcium and magnesium ions, forming a stable complex. The endpoint of the titration is reached when the color of the solution changes from wine red to blue. This indicates that all the calcium and magnesium ions have been complexed with EDTA.

3. Note down the volume of EDTA solution used for titration. The amount of EDTA used will be equal to the amount of calcium and magnesium ions present in the water sample.

4. Calculate the hardness of water sample in terms of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) using the following formula: 

    Hardness (in ppm) = (Volume of EDTA solution used x Molarity of EDTA solution x 100) / Volume of water sample taken

    Hardness (in mg/L) = Hardness (in ppm) / 10

    Hardness (in °dH) = Hardness (in mg/L) / 0.1

    where Molarity of EDTA solution = (Molecular weight of EDTA x Normality of EDTA solution) / 1000.

5. Repeat the above steps for multiple water samples to obtain an average hardness value. 

6. Compare the obtained hardness value with the permissible limits set by the regulatory authorities. 

In conclusion, the determination of hardness of water sample by EDTA method is a simple and effective method to determine the amount of calcium and magnesium ions present in the water sample. This method provides accurate results and is widely used in water quality analysis.



### 3. Determination of Alkalinity of Water Sample

Alkalinity is an important parameter that indicates the ability of water to neutralize acids. It is a measure of the amount of alkaline substances present in water, such as bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide ions. The alkalinity of water is important in many applications, such as agriculture, industry, and domestic use. The determination of alkalinity is therefore an important test in the analysis of water quality.

The following are the steps involved in the determination of alkalinity of a water sample:

1. Collect the water sample: Collect a representative sample of the water to be tested in a clean container. The amount of water required for the test depends on the method used for the determination of alkalinity.

2. Add the indicator: Add a few drops of the indicator to the water sample. The most commonly used indicator for the determination of alkalinity is phenolphthalein. The indicator changes color to pink when the water sample reaches a pH of around 8.3.

3. Titrate the water sample: Titrate the water sample with a standardized acid solution until the endpoint is reached. The endpoint is the point at which the indicator changes color. In the case of phenolphthalein, the endpoint is reached when the water sample turns from pink to colorless.

4. Record the volume of acid used: Record the volume of the acid solution used to reach the endpoint. This volume is used to calculate the alkalinity of the water sample.

5. Calculate the alkalinity: Calculate the alkalinity of the water sample using the formula:

Alkalinity = (Volume of acid used x Normality of acid) x 50 / Volume of water sample

where:
- Volume of acid used is the volume of the standardized acid solution used to reach the endpoint.
- Normality of acid is the concentration of the standardized acid solution in normal units.
- Volume of water sample is the volume of the water sample used for the test in milliliters.

6. Interpret the results: The alkalinity of the water sample is expressed in units of mg/L or ppm of calcium carbonate (CaCO3). The acceptable range of alkalinity for drinking water is between 20-200 ppm.

In conclusion, the determination of alkalinity is an important test in the analysis of water quality. It is a measure of the ability of water to neutralize acids and is important in many applications. The steps involved in the determination of alkalinity are simple and straightforward, and the results can be easily interpreted.



### 4. Determination of pH by titrimetric method.

The determination of pH is an important aspect in many fields of chemistry and biology. The pH of a solution can be determined by various methods, one of which is the titrimetric method. In this method, a solution of known concentration is added to the solution being tested until the pH of the solution reaches a particular value. The pH can then be calculated using the known concentration of the solution and the volume of the solution added.

The titrimetric method for the determination of pH involves the following steps:

1. Prepare the solution being tested: The solution being tested should be prepared by dissolving the substance of interest in a suitable solvent. The solution should be stirred well to ensure that the substance is completely dissolved.

2. Add the indicator: An indicator is added to the solution being tested. The indicator is a substance that changes color in response to changes in pH. The choice of indicator depends on the pH range being tested.

3. Add the titrant: The titrant is a solution of known concentration that is added to the solution being tested. The titrant should be added slowly and with constant stirring until the pH of the solution reaches the desired value. The volume of the titrant added is recorded.

4. Calculate the pH: The pH of the solution can be calculated using the known concentration of the titrant, the volume of the titrant added, and the volume of the solution being tested.

5. Repeat the titration: The titration should be repeated at least two more times to ensure accuracy. The average of the three titrations should be used to calculate the pH.

6. Record the results: The pH of the solution should be recorded along with any relevant observations.

Some important points to keep in mind while performing titrimetric determination of pH are:

- The choice of indicator depends on the pH range being tested. For example, phenolphthalein is suitable for pH range of 8.3 to 10.0, while methyl orange is suitable for pH range of 3.1 to 4.4.

- The titrant should be added slowly and with constant stirring to ensure that the pH of the solution is accurately measured.

- It is important to repeat the titration to ensure accuracy.

- The pH of the solution should be recorded along with any relevant observations.

In conclusion, the titrimetric method is a reliable and accurate method for the determination of pH. The method involves the addition of a solution of known concentration to the solution being tested until the pH of the solution reaches a particular value. The pH can then be calculated using the known concentration of the solution and the volume of the solution added. Careful attention to the choice of indicator, the addition of the titrant, and the repetition of the titration can ensure accurate results.



### 5. Determination of Surface Tension of Given Liquid

Surface tension is a phenomenon that occurs at the interface of two immiscible phases, such as liquid-liquid or liquid-gas. It is defined as the force per unit length acting perpendicular to an imaginary line drawn on the surface of the liquid, and is measured in units of N/m. Surface tension plays an important role in a number of physical and biological processes, such as capillary action, wetting, and droplet formation.

The determination of surface tension of a given liquid can be carried out using various methods. Here are some of the commonly used techniques:

1. Capillary rise method: In this method, a narrow glass tube of known radius is immersed in the liquid, and the height to which the liquid rises in the tube is measured. The surface tension of the liquid can be calculated using the formula T = (2gh)/r, where T is the surface tension, h is the height of liquid rise, g is the acceleration due to gravity, and r is the radius of the tube.

2. Drop weight method: In this method, a drop of the liquid is allowed to fall from a height onto a horizontal surface, and the diameter of the resulting circular spot is measured. The surface tension of the liquid can be calculated using the formula T = (4mgh)/(πd^2), where T is the surface tension, m is the mass of the drop, g is the acceleration due to gravity, h is the height of fall, and d is the diameter of the spot.

3. Maximum bubble pressure method: In this method, a soap film is formed by blowing air through a soap solution, and the pressure required to burst the film is measured. The surface tension of the liquid can be calculated using the formula T = (4P)/(d), where T is the surface tension, P is the pressure required to burst the film, and d is the diameter of the soap bubble.

4. Wilhelmy plate method: In this method, a thin rectangular plate of known dimensions is partially immersed in the liquid, and the force required to detach the plate from the liquid surface is measured. The surface tension of the liquid can be calculated using the formula T = (Fcosθ)/l, where T is the surface tension, F is the force required to detach the plate, θ is the contact angle between the plate and the liquid surface, and l is the length of the plate.

It is important to note that the determination of surface tension using any of these methods requires careful experimental setup and measurement, as well as consideration of factors such as temperature, atmospheric pressure, and impurities in the liquid. Additionally, the choice of method may depend on the properties of the liquid being studied and the desired accuracy of the measurement.



### 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer.

Viscosity is a measure of a liquid's resistance to flow. It is an important physical property that is used to describe the behavior of fluids. The viscosity of a liquid is affected by several factors such as temperature, pressure, and the nature of the liquid.

Viscometers are instruments used to measure the viscosity of liquids. There are different types of viscometers available, but the most commonly used ones are the capillary viscometers. In this experiment, we will determine the viscosity of a given liquid using a capillary viscometer.

The steps involved in determining the viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer are:

1. Set up the apparatus: The apparatus consists of a viscometer, a thermometer, a constant temperature bath, and a stopwatch. The viscometer consists of a U-shaped glass tube with a bulb at one end. The tube is filled with the liquid to be tested and the bulb is sealed. The thermometer is used to measure the temperature of the liquid.

2. Measure the temperature: The temperature of the liquid should be measured using the thermometer. The temperature should be constant throughout the experiment.

3. Measure the time: The stopwatch is used to measure the time taken for the liquid to flow from one end of the viscometer to the other. The time should be measured accurately.

4. Calculate the viscosity: The viscosity of the liquid can be calculated using the formula: viscosity = K * (t - t0), where K is the constant of the viscometer, t is the time taken for the liquid to flow through the viscometer, and t0 is the time taken for the liquid to flow through the viscometer when the liquid is replaced with a reference liquid of known viscosity.

5. Repeat the experiment: The experiment should be repeated several times to obtain an average value of viscosity. The temperature of the liquid should also be varied to observe the effect of temperature on viscosity.

6. Record the results: The results should be recorded in a tabular form, showing the viscosity of the liquid at different temperatures.

In conclusion, the determination of viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer is an important experiment that helps in understanding the physical properties of liquids. The experiment should be carried out carefully and accurately to obtain reliable results.



### 7. Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator.

Ferrous ammonium sulfate is a chemical compound commonly used in analytical chemistry as a reducing agent, especially in the determination of the concentration of potassium permanganate. The strength of this compound can be determined by using an external indicator, which is a substance that changes color in response to the presence of a certain chemical. Here are the steps for determining the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using an external indicator:

1. Prepare a standard solution of potassium permanganate of known concentration.
2. Weigh accurately about 0.2 g of Ferrous ammonium sulfate and dissolve it in distilled water in a 250 ml volumetric flask.
3. Add a few drops of dilute sulfuric acid to the solution to prevent the oxidation of the Ferrous ion.
4. Add a few crystals of the external indicator, which is generally potassium ferricyanide. The indicator will react with the Ferrous ion in the solution and form a blue-colored complex.
5. Titrate the solution with the standard solution of potassium permanganate until the blue color disappears. This indicates that all of the Ferrous ion has been oxidized to Ferric ion by the potassium permanganate.
6. Record the volume of the standard solution required for titration.
7. Repeat the above steps two more times and calculate the average volume of the standard solution required for titration.
8. From the volume of the standard solution required for titration, calculate the strength of the Ferrous ammonium sulfate solution in terms of grams per liter.

It is important to note that the external indicator used in this method should be carefully chosen to ensure that it does not interfere with the reaction between Ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium permanganate. Additionally, the solution should be titrated immediately after the addition of the external indicator to avoid any color changes due to oxidation of the Ferrous ion.

In conclusion, the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate can be accurately determined using an external indicator and a standard solution of potassium permanganate. This method is commonly used in analytical chemistry and is an important technique for determining the concentration of reducing agents.



### 8. Determination of the Strength of Potassium Dichromate using Internal Indicator

Potassium dichromate is a common oxidizing agent used in many chemical reactions. It is important to determine the strength of the solution to ensure that the correct amount is used in a reaction. The strength of potassium dichromate can be determined using an internal indicator.

Here are the steps involved in the determination of the strength of potassium dichromate using an internal indicator:

1. Prepare the potassium dichromate solution by dissolving a known amount of potassium dichromate in distilled water. The concentration of the solution should be around 0.1M.

2. Add a small amount of an internal indicator to the solution. The internal indicator used is diphenylamine. The amount of the indicator added should be around 0.1g.

3. Titrate the solution against a standard solution of ferrous ammonium sulfate. The ferrous ammonium sulfate solution should also be prepared with a concentration of 0.1M.

4. The titration should be carried out in the presence of sulfuric acid. The concentration of sulfuric acid used should be around 1M.

5. During the titration, the potassium dichromate solution will react with the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution. The diphenylamine indicator will change color from blue to green when all the ferrous ammonium sulfate is consumed.

6. Note down the volume of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution required to reach the end point of the titration. The volume of the potassium dichromate solution used for the titration should also be noted.

7. Calculate the strength of the potassium dichromate solution using the formula:

Strength of potassium dichromate = (Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution used x Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution)/Volume of potassium dichromate solution used

8. Repeat the titration two more times to obtain an average value for the strength of the potassium dichromate solution.

9. Record all the observations and calculations in a tabular form.

By following these steps, the strength of potassium dichromate can be determined accurately using an internal indicator. This method is widely used in laboratories for the analysis of chemical compounds.



### 9. Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder.

Bleaching powder is a white or pale yellow powder that is commonly used as a bleaching agent, disinfectant, and as a source of chlorine in various chemical processes. The available chlorine in bleaching powder is the amount of chlorine that can be released from the powder when it is dissolved in water. The determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder is an important analytical test that is used to ensure the quality of the product.

The following are the steps involved in the determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder:

1. Preparation of the sample: Weigh accurately about 2 grams of bleaching powder sample and dissolve it in 100 ml of distilled water. Mix the solution thoroughly and filter it through a filter paper to remove any insoluble impurities.

2. Standardization of the sodium thiosulfate solution: Prepare a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate by dissolving 25 g of pure sodium thiosulfate in 1 liter of distilled water. Add a few drops of a starch solution to the standard solution. Titrate the standard solution against 0.1 N iodine solution until a blue color appears. Note down the volume of iodine solution used for the titration.

3. Titration: Add 10 ml of 10% potassium iodide solution to the filtered bleaching powder solution. Then add 10 ml of 1 N sulfuric acid to the solution. Titrate the solution against the standard sodium thiosulfate solution until the yellow color disappears. Add a few drops of starch solution to the solution and continue the titration until the blue color disappears. Note down the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used for the titration.

4. Calculation: The available chlorine in the bleaching powder sample can be calculated using the following formula:

Available chlorine (%) = (volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used × normality of sodium thiosulfate solution × 0.0035) / weight of the sample (in grams)

Where 0.0035 is the equivalent weight of available chlorine.

In conclusion, the determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder is a simple and important analytical test that is used to ensure the quality of the product. By following the above steps, the available chlorine content in the bleaching powder sample can be accurately determined.



### 10. Determination of Chloride Content in Water Sample

In analytical chemistry, the determination of chloride content in water samples is an important procedure. Chloride ions are commonly found in natural and synthetic water sources, and their concentration can be indicative of water quality. The following points outline the procedure for the determination of chloride content in a water sample:

1. Sample Collection: Collect a representative sample of the water to be tested. The sample should be collected in a clean, sterile container to avoid contamination.

2. Sample Preparation: Filter the water sample using a filter paper to remove any particulate matter. This will ensure accurate results.

3. Reagent Preparation: Prepare a silver nitrate solution by dissolving an accurate amount of silver nitrate in distilled water. This solution will be used to precipitate the chloride ions.

4. Titration: Add a few drops of potassium chromate indicator to the filtered water sample. Then, slowly add the silver nitrate solution until a reddish-brown color appears. This indicates the endpoint of the titration.

5. Calculation: Calculate the chloride ion concentration using the following formula:

   Chloride concentration (mg/L) = (ml of AgNO3 solution used x normality of AgNO3 solution x 35.5) / ml of water sample

   Where 35.5 is the molecular weight of chloride ion and normality of AgNO3 solution is calculated as follows:

   Normality of AgNO3 solution = (molarity of AgNO3 solution x 35.5) / 1000

   Molarity of AgNO3 solution is the concentration of the silver nitrate solution in moles per liter.

6. Quality Control: Perform quality control checks to ensure that the results are within acceptable limits. This can be done by analyzing a certified reference material or by comparing the results with those obtained by another analytical method.

In conclusion, the determination of chloride content in water samples is a critical procedure that requires careful sample collection, preparation, and analysis. By following the above procedure, accurate and reliable results can be obtained.



### 11. Preparation of Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin

Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in various industries, such as construction, automotive, and electronics. It is a synthetic resin that is formed by the reaction of phenol (an aromatic organic compound) and formaldehyde (a simple organic compound). In this section, we will discuss the preparation process of PF resin.

The preparation of PF resin involves the following steps:

1. Mixing Phenol and Formaldehyde: Phenol and formaldehyde are mixed together in a reactor vessel. The ratio of phenol to formaldehyde varies depending on the desired properties of the PF resin.

2. Addition of Catalyst: A catalyst is added to the mixture to initiate the reaction between phenol and formaldehyde. The most commonly used catalyst is an alkali metal hydroxide, such as sodium hydroxide (NaOH) or potassium hydroxide (KOH).

3. Heating and Stirring: The mixture is heated and stirred to facilitate the reaction between phenol and formaldehyde. The temperature and duration of the reaction depend on the desired properties of the PF resin.

4. Cooling and Neutralization: The reaction is stopped by cooling the mixture and neutralizing it with an acid, such as hydrochloric acid (HCl) or sulfuric acid (H2SO4). This step is important to prevent the resin from over-polymerizing.

5. Filtration and Washing: The PF resin is then filtered to remove any impurities and washed with water to remove any remaining chemicals.

6. Drying: Finally, the PF resin is dried to remove any moisture and to ensure that it is in a stable, solid form.

In conclusion, the preparation of PF resin is a complex process that requires careful control of the reaction conditions to achieve the desired properties of the resin. The resulting PF resin is a versatile material that has a wide range of applications in various industries.



### 12. Preparation of Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin

Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer made by the reaction of urea and formaldehyde. It is widely used in various industries such as furniture, construction, and automotive for its excellent properties like high strength, durability, and resistance to heat and water. The preparation of UF resin involves the following steps:

1. Preparation of formaldehyde solution: Formaldehyde is used in the form of an aqueous solution, which is prepared by adding formaldehyde gas to water. The concentration of formaldehyde in the solution depends on the desired properties of the resin.

2. Preparation of urea solution: Urea is dissolved in water to form a solution. The concentration of urea in the solution also affects the properties of the final resin.

3. Mixing of formaldehyde and urea solutions: Formaldehyde solution is slowly added to the urea solution with constant stirring. The pH of the mixture is adjusted to around 8.5 to facilitate the reaction.

4. Condensation reaction: The mixture is heated to initiate the condensation reaction between urea and formaldehyde. The reaction produces water as a byproduct and forms a three-dimensional polymer network.

5. Post-condensation: After the initial condensation, the mixture is further heated to promote the formation of cross-links between the polymer chains. This step improves the properties of the resin, such as its strength and hardness.

6. Neutralization: The pH of the mixture is reduced to neutral by adding an acid to stop the reaction and stabilize the resin.

7. Filtering and drying: The resin is filtered to remove any impurities and then dried to remove excess water.

8. Storage: The UF resin is stored in a cool and dry place for future use.

In conclusion, the preparation of UF resin involves the reaction of urea and formaldehyde, which is initiated by heating and results in a thermosetting polymer with excellent properties. The process involves careful control of the concentration of reactants, pH, and temperature to obtain the desired properties of the resin.



### 13. Preparation of Adipic acid / Paracetamol

Adipic acid and Paracetamol are two important chemicals used in various industries. Here are the methods for their preparation:

#### Preparation of Adipic acid:

1. Oxidation of Cyclohexane: Cyclohexane is oxidized using nitric acid and air to produce adipic acid. The reaction is exothermic and produces a large amount of heat. The yield of adipic acid is around 60-70%.

2. Oxidation of Cyclohexene: Cyclohexene is oxidized using potassium permanganate and sulfuric acid to produce adipic acid. The yield is around 90%.

3. Oxidation of Cyclohexanol: Cyclohexanol is oxidized using sodium hypochlorite or potassium dichromate to produce adipic acid. The yield is around 70-80%.

#### Preparation of Paracetamol:

1. Nitration of Phenol: Phenol is nitrated using nitric acid and sulfuric acid to produce 4-nitrophenol. The yield is around 90%. 

2. Reduction of 4-nitrophenol: 4-nitrophenol is reduced using sodium borohydride to produce 4-aminophenol. The yield is around 80-90%.

3. Acetylation of 4-aminophenol: 4-aminophenol is acetylated using acetic anhydride to produce Paracetamol. The yield is around 80-90%.

Note: The above methods are for educational purposes only. Professionals should always follow safety protocols and regulations while conducting chemical reactions.



### 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

Cell conductance is the measure of the ability of a solution to conduct electricity. It is an important parameter in electrochemistry and is used to determine the concentration of ions in a solution. The following points outline the methods and procedures for determining the cell conductance of a solution:

1. Select a suitable cell: A conductivity cell is used to measure the cell conductance of a solution. The cell consists of two electrodes, a reference electrode, and a measuring electrode. A glass conductivity cell is commonly used for this purpose.

2. Calibration of the cell: Before measuring the conductance of the solution, the cell must be calibrated using a standard solution of known conductivity. This is done to ensure accurate measurements.

3. Preparation of the sample solution: The sample solution must be prepared by dissolving the required amount of solute in a solvent. The solution must be stirred well to ensure uniformity.

4. Temperature control: The temperature of the solution must be maintained at a constant value throughout the measurement process. This is important because the conductance of a solution is dependent on temperature.

5. Measurement of cell conductance: The conductivity cell is immersed in the solution, and the conductance of the solution is measured. The conductance is measured in siemens (S) or mhos (℧).

6. Calculation of cell conductance: The cell conductance is calculated using the formula:

    Cell conductance = (measured conductance × cell constant) / cell length

    where cell constant is the factor that relates the conductance of the solution to the conductance of the cell, and cell length is the length of the cell.

7. Determination of concentration: The cell conductance is used to determine the concentration of ions in the solution. The concentration can be calculated using standard tables or graphs.

In conclusion, the determination of cell conductance is an important procedure in electrochemistry. The process involves the selection of a suitable cell, calibration of the cell, preparation of the sample solution, temperature control, measurement of cell conductance, calculation of cell conductance, and determination of concentration. Accurate measurements of cell conductance can be used to determine the concentration of ions in a solution, which is important in many scientific and industrial applications.



### 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

In this experiment, we will determine the rate constant of hydrolysis of ethyl acetate using alkaline hydroxide solution. The reaction is given as follows:

Ethyl acetate + Hydroxide ion → Ethanol + Acetate ion

The rate of the reaction can be expressed as:

Rate = k [Ethyl acetate] [Hydroxide ion]

Where k is the rate constant of the reaction.

**Experimental Procedure:**

1. Prepare a solution of 0.1 M sodium hydroxide (NaOH) by dissolving 4 g of NaOH in 100 mL of distilled water.

2. In a 250 mL beaker, take 25 mL of 0.1 M NaOH solution.

3. Add a few drops of phenolphthalein indicator to the solution. The solution will turn pink.

4. Take 5 mL of ethyl acetate in a clean and dry test tube.

5. Add 20 mL of distilled water to the test tube.

6. Cover the test tube with a stopper and shake it vigorously for 1 minute.

7. Quickly add the ethyl acetate solution to the NaOH solution in the beaker and start the stop watch.

8. Stir the solution and observe the change in colour from pink to colourless.

9. Note down the time taken for the reaction to complete.

10. Repeat the experiment with different concentrations of ethyl acetate solution (2 mL, 3 mL, 4 mL, 5 mL) keeping the volume of NaOH solution constant.

**Calculations:**

1. Calculate the moles of NaOH used in the reaction.

2. Calculate the moles of ethyl acetate used in the reaction.

3. Calculate the initial concentration of ethyl acetate.

4. Calculate the rate constant (k) using the formula:

k = 2.303/t log (C0/Ct)

Where t is the time taken for the reaction to complete, C0 is the initial concentration of ethyl acetate, and Ct is the concentration of ethyl acetate at time t.

**Precautions:**

1. Use clean and dry glassware for the experiment.

2. Use distilled water for making the solutions.

3. Do not touch the NaOH solution with bare hands.

4. Use a stop watch to measure the time accurately.

5. Keep the volume of NaOH solution constant while changing the concentration of ethyl acetate solution.

6. Perform the experiment in a well-ventilated area.

7. Dispose of the waste properly.

In conclusion, the determination of the rate constant of hydrolysis of esters using alkaline hydroxide solution is an important experiment in physical chemistry. It involves careful preparation of solutions and accurate measurement of time to determine the rate constant of the reaction. By following the experimental procedure and taking necessary precautions, accurate results can be obtained.



### 16. Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

Organic compounds are carbon-based molecules that contain functional groups, which are responsible for their chemical properties and reactivity. In order to identify these functional groups and determine the elements present in an organic compound, various analytical techniques can be employed. Some of these techniques are:

1. Elemental analysis: This technique involves determining the elemental composition of a compound by measuring the amounts of carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, and other elements present. This can be done using techniques such as combustion analysis, which involves burning the compound in a stream of oxygen and measuring the products of combustion.

2. Infrared spectroscopy (IR): IR spectroscopy is a technique that can be used to identify functional groups in organic compounds. This technique measures the absorption of infrared radiation by the compound, which is related to the vibrations of the atoms in the molecule. Different functional groups absorb at different frequencies, allowing for their identification.

3. Nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) spectroscopy: NMR spectroscopy is another technique used to identify the functional groups present in organic compounds. This technique measures the magnetic properties of the nuclei in the molecule, which are related to the local chemical environment. By analyzing the NMR spectrum, information about the types of functional groups and their arrangement in the molecule can be obtained.

4. Mass spectrometry (MS): MS is a technique that can be used to determine the mass of a molecule and its fragmentation pattern. By analyzing the fragmentation pattern, information about the functional groups and their arrangement in the molecule can be obtained.

5. Chromatography: Chromatography is a separation technique that can be used to separate and isolate the components of a mixture. Different types of chromatography, such as gas chromatography and liquid chromatography, can be employed depending on the properties of the compounds being analyzed.

In conclusion, the identification of functional groups and elements in organic compounds is important for understanding their chemical properties and reactivity. Various analytical techniques can be employed to achieve this, including elemental analysis, IR spectroscopy, NMR spectroscopy, MS, and chromatography.



### NOTE: Instructor may choose any 10 experiments from above and may also change any two of the above..

In the field of science, experiments play a pivotal role in advancing our knowledge and understanding of the natural world. As a student, it is essential to have a firm grasp of experimental design, methodology, and data analysis. The following points provide a brief overview of ten different experiments that instructors may choose to teach in their courses:

1. Millikan's Oil Drop Experiment: This experiment determined the charge of an electron and was critical in advancing the understanding of atomic structure.

2. Pavlov's Classical Conditioning Experiment: This experiment demonstrated how animals learn through association and has profound implications for animal behavior and psychology.

3. Watson and Rayner's Little Albert Experiment: This experiment showed how fear could be conditioned in humans and has become a classic example of ethical concerns in research.

4. Asch's Conformity Experiment: This experiment demonstrated how people are influenced by social pressure and has important implications for group dynamics and decision-making.

5. Milgram's Obedience Experiment: This experiment investigated the willingness of people to obey authority figures, even when it involved inflicting harm on others.

6. Harlow's Monkey Experiment: This experiment examined the importance of maternal attachment and socialization in primates and has had significant implications for child development and psychology.

7. Skinner's Operant Conditioning Experiment: This experiment demonstrated how behavior can be shaped through positive and negative reinforcement and has implications for education, parenting, and training.

8. Zimbardo's Stanford Prison Experiment: This experiment investigated the role of power and authority in shaping human behavior and has important implications for ethics in research and the legal system.

9. Rosenhan's On Being Sane in Insane Places Experiment: This experiment demonstrated how psychiatric diagnoses can be influenced by social and environmental factors and has had significant implications for mental health treatment and policy.

10. Loftus and Palmer's Car Crash Experiment: This experiment investigated how memory can be influenced by leading questions and has important implications for eyewitness testimony and the legal system.

Instructors may also choose to modify the above experiments or select different experiments altogether. Regardless of the specific experiments chosen, it is important for students to understand the fundamental principles of scientific inquiry and experimental design. By studying these experiments, students can develop a deep understanding of the scientific method and its role in advancing our knowledge of the natural world.
